My wife says I leave things half done sometimes.\n\nSo… maybe there are things, but I haven’t really kept a list in my head of what I don’t want to do anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time, even when there's no apparent reason to be anxious?\n\nPatient: Most of the time? I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, sometimes I’ll feel… keyed up, I guess, and I couldn’t tell you exactly why. But then I’ll be sitting there and notice the clock is ticking loud, or I’ll remember I forgot to move the laundry, and then I’m thinking about that instead.\n\nIt’s hard to separate out what’s “no reason” and what’s just normal things piling up. My wife used to say I’d get wound up over little things, but then again she’d also say I couldn’t find the ketchup if it was right in front of me, so… I don’t know. I can’t give you a clean answer on “most of the time.”\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel anxious for no specific reason when you are alone?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. When I’m alone… I don’t know. Sometimes the house feels quieter than I expect, and then I start noticing things, like the refrigerator humming or a car going by too slow, and I’ll wonder who that is, but then it’s probably just the neighbor’s son. He drives one of those loud little cars—my grandson was telling me they tune the exhaust on purpose now, which I don’t understand.

---------- turn 18 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F41.0, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I came in because I’m not handling things right. They changed my hours at the warehouse, and then put in this new inventory system—computers and scanners and all that—and ever since, I feel like I’m just… coming apart.\n\nI used to be pretty dependable, you know. I’d show up, do my stocking, fill orders, keep my head down. I’ve always been that way—even back when I was younger, I took pride in being on time. But lately I’ve been missing shifts, and even missing things at church I said I’d help with. My wife says I’ve been snapping at everybody, and she’s not wrong.\n\nSome mornings I’ll get dressed, have my work boots on, and just sit there like I can’t make myself go in. It sounds foolish when I say it, but that place just feels like too much now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel nervous or on edge most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose I do, but it’s mostly tied to that warehouse situation. Ever since they changed my hours and put in that new inventory system with the scanners, I just feel like I’m falling apart.\n\nI mean, I’ll start thinking about going in and then my mind runs off—like whether I’ll mess up the orders, or if the supervisor’s watching. Then I think about how I used to never miss a shift, and now I’m missing work and even things at church I promised I’d help with. My wife says I’m short with everybody lately, and she’s right.\n\nSome mornings I’m sitting there in my work boots, just staring, wondering if I can even face the place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or hopeless when you're not thinking about work or the warehouse?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, when I’m away from work, sometimes I can sit with my wife or watch the game and be mostly alright… though my mind does wander back.\n\nHopeless, though… I don’t know. That’s a strong word. My father used to say people throw words around too easy—he worked hard right up until his knees gave out, and he never talked about feelings much. I guess I learned that from him.\n\nSo… I don’t really know if I feel sad when it’s not about the warehouse. I mostly just know that whole situation follows me around.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel nervous or on edge when you're not thinking about work or the warehouse?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed it separate from work, to be honest. It’s hard to pull the two apart, because once I’m home I’ll be sitting there and something small reminds me of the warehouse anyway.\n\nLike the other night my wife was asking where we keep the extra batteries, and I started thinking about those scanner batteries at work—how they never seem charged when you need them—and then I’m back there in my head. So… nervous when I’m not thinking about it? I’m not sure. I’d have to pay more attention, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day, even when you're not thinking about work or the warehouse?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say most of the day, no… or—I mean, I don’t really track it like that.\n\nSome days I feel worn down, but then I’ll get distracted by regular things, you know, fixing something around the house or talking with my wife, and it’s not like I’m sitting there crying all day. My mother used to keep a little notebook for everything—appointments, grocery prices, who called—and maybe if I did that I could tell you better. I just kind of go through the day.\n\nSo… sad most of the day when I’m not thinking about the warehouse? I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed it that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to enjoy doing that you don't find pleasurable anymore?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, I still do things, but whether I enjoy them the same way… I don’t know.\n\nI used to like tinkering around in the garage, but then again half the time I’d go out there just because something needed fixing. My neighbor had this old lawnmower once—wouldn’t start no matter what—and I spent an entire Saturday with him taking it apart. Funny thing is, it turned out to be something simple, like the fuel line, I think. He still brings that up sometimes.\n\nBut as far as not finding anything pleasurable anymore… I don’t know. I haven’t really sat down and measured it like that. Some things feel different, maybe, but I couldn’t tell you cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to look forward to that you no longer look forward to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I used to look forward to little things, I guess, but when you ask it that way my mind kind of goes blank.\n\nLike, there’s this diner my wife and I used to go to—well, we still go sometimes—out by the old hardware store. They changed owners a while back, and the coffee’s not the same. My wife says I’m too particular about coffee, and she’s probably right. My father was like that too, always complaining if it sat on the burner too long.\n\nBut whether I don’t look forward to things anymore… I don’t know. Some days I just don’t think that far ahead. I haven’t really paid attention to it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding places or situations outside of work because they remind you of the warehouse?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really thought of it like avoiding places.\n\nI mean, outside of work I still go where I need to go—store, gas station, church sometimes if my wife’s going. Though there are places with those big loading bays, you know, the kind behind grocery stores, and they all sort of look the same after a while. Funny thing, when I was younger I worked a summer at a place that smelled like cardboard and floor cleaner, and every time I smell that same kind of cleaner I think of it.\n\nBut avoiding them because of the warehouse… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve been keeping track of that. I might go the long way sometimes just out of habit, but I couldn’t say it’s because of that exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you no longer enjoy activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to say it. I mean, I still do things I used to do, but whether I enjoy them like before… I haven’t kept track of it that way.\n\nSometimes I’ll start something, like sorting through stuff in the garage, and then I end up just moving the same box from one side to the other. There was a box of old fishing gear out there the other day—my brother gave me some of that years back—and I started thinking about how we used to argue over who got the better rod. He always said I tangled the line on purpose, which wasn’t true.\n\nSo, do I no longer enjoy things? I’m not sure. Some things don’t feel the same maybe, but I couldn’t give you a straight yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed anything significant. My pants fit about the same, I think, though some of them are old work pants and they’ve been stretched out for years.\n\nMy wife bought a scale a while back, one of those digital ones that flashes numbers at you, but I don’t get on it much. I remember my mother had one with the little needle, and you had to tap it with your foot to make it go back to zero. Those were simpler, I guess.\n\nBut, no… I can’t say I’ve noticed a big change in my weight recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you are not thinking about work or the warehouse?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out that cleanly. I mean, if I’m not thinking about work, I’m usually thinking about something else—like bills, or whether I locked the back door, or some little thing my wife asked me to pick up and I forgot until I’m already sitting down.\n\nAnxious… that’s a word people use more now than they used to, I think. When I was younger we just said we were “wound up” or “had nerves,” like my aunt used to say. She’d keep peppermint candies in her purse and offer you one if you looked upset, as if that fixed everything.\n\nSo, do I often feel anxious when I’m not thinking about the warehouse? I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed it in a way I could point to. Maybe sometimes I feel uneasy, but I couldn’t tell you it’s happening often or what it’s tied to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're doing things that have nothing to do with the warehouse or your job?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m not really sure. If I’m doing something completely separate from work, like sitting at home or fiddling with something in the yard, I don’t always stop and think, “Am I anxious right now?” It’s more like I’m just doing the thing.\n\nSometimes my mind wanders off, though. I’ll be fixing a hinge or looking for a screwdriver, and then I remember my father had this old coffee can full of screws, all different sizes, and he somehow knew exactly which one he needed. I’ve got jars of them now, but half the time I can’t find the right one.\n\nSo… anxious during things that don’t have to do with the warehouse? I couldn’t say for sure. I haven’t really noticed it clearly enough to answer yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are relaxing or doing leisure activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Relaxing is a funny word, isn’t it? I sit in my chair sometimes, or I’ll put something on the television, but half the time I’m not paying much attention to it. My wife likes those cooking shows where they chop everything up ahead of time in little bowls. We never did that—my mother would just cut things right over the pot.\n\nBut anxious while I’m relaxing… I’m not sure I’d call it that. I don’t always notice what I’m feeling in the moment. Sometimes I’m just sitting there, and then I realize I’ve been staring at the same thing for a while. I couldn’t say for certain whether it’s anxiety or just me being older and distracted.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you are with your family or friends?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve paid attention to it like that. When I’m with my family, I’m usually trying to listen, or keep up with what the grandkids are saying—if they’re there, they talk so fast now. Half the time they’re showing me something on a phone and I can’t even tell where I’m supposed to look.\n\nWith friends, well… I don’t see as many people as I used to. Everybody gets older, people move, people get busy. We used to sit around after work sometimes, but that was years ago, and there was this diner that had pie in a glass case—lemon meringue, coconut cream, all that. You don’t see those places much anymore.\n\nSo, anxious with family or friends… I couldn’t really say “often.” I’m not sure I’d notice it unless someone pointed it out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you are not thinking about work or anything related to the warehouse?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. If I’m not thinking about work, I don’t know that I stop and check whether I’m anxious or not. Sometimes I’ll be thinking about something completely different, like whether the gutters need cleaning, or I’ll notice the neighbor’s dog barking and then I’m off remembering the dog we had when the kids were little.\n\nSo… when it’s not about the warehouse, I can’t really give you a clean yes or no. I haven’t noticed it clearly enough.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do for fun that you no longer want to do?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that. “Fun” changes a lot when you get older, doesn’t it? I used to do different things, sure, but some of that is just because life changes. You don’t run around the way you used to.\n\nI’ll start something sometimes and then not stick with it, but I can’t say if that’s because I don’t want to do it anymore or just because I get sidetracked. Like I’ll think about watching a game, and then I’m looking for the remote, and then I notice a stack of mail on the table. My wife says I leave things half done sometimes.\n\nSo… maybe there are things, but I haven’t really kept a list in my head of what I don’t want to do anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time, even when there's no apparent reason to be anxious?\n\nPatient: Most of the time? I mean, I still do things I used to do, but whether I enjoy them like before… I haven’t kept track of it that way.\n\nSometimes I’ll start something, like sorting through stuff in the garage, and then I end up just moving the same box from one side to the other. There was a box of old fishing gear out there the other day—my brother gave me some of that years back—and I started thinking about how we used to argue over who got the better rod. He always said I tangled the line on purpose, which wasn’t true.\n\nSo, do I no longer enjoy things? I’m not sure. Some things don’t feel the same maybe, but I couldn’t give you a straight yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed anything significant. My pants fit about the same, I think, though some of them are old work pants and they’ve been stretched out for years.\n\nMy wife bought a scale a while back, one of those digital ones that flashes numbers at you, but I don’t get on it much. I remember my mother had one with the little needle, and you had to tap it with your foot to make it go back to zero. Those were simpler, I guess.\n\nBut, no… I can’t say I’ve noticed a big change in my weight recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you are not thinking about work or the warehouse?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out that cleanly. I mean, if I’m not thinking about work, I’m usually thinking about something else—like bills, or whether I locked the back door, or some little thing my wife asked me to pick up and I forgot until I’m already sitting down.\n\nAnxious… that’s a word people use more now than they used to, I think. When I was younger we just said we were “wound up” or “had nerves,” like my aunt used to say. She’d keep peppermint candies in her purse and offer you one if you looked upset, as if that fixed everything.\n\nSo, do I often feel anxious when I’m not thinking about the warehouse? I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed it in a way I could point to. Maybe sometimes I feel uneasy, but I couldn’t tell you it’s happening often or what it’s tied to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're doing things that have nothing to do with the warehouse or your job?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m not really sure. If I’m doing something completely separate from work, like sitting at home or fiddling with something in the yard, I don’t always stop and think, “Am I anxious right now?” It’s more like I’m just doing the thing.\n\nSometimes my mind wanders off, though. I’ll be fixing a hinge or looking for a screwdriver, and then I remember my father had this old coffee can full of screws, all different sizes, and he somehow knew exactly which one he needed. I’ve got jars of them now, but half the time I can’t find the right one.\n\nSo… anxious during things that don’t have to do with the warehouse? I couldn’t say for sure. I haven’t really noticed it clearly enough to answer yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are relaxing or doing leisure activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Relaxing is a funny word, isn’t it? I sit in my chair sometimes, or I’ll put something on the television, but half the time I’m not paying much attention to it. My wife likes those cooking shows where they chop everything up ahead of time in little bowls. We never did that—my mother would just cut things right over the pot.\n\nBut anxious while I’m relaxing… I’m not sure I’d call it that. I don’t always notice what I’m feeling in the moment. Sometimes I’m just sitting there, and then I realize I’ve been staring at the same thing for a while. I couldn’t say for certain whether it’s anxiety or just me being older and distracted.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you are with your family or friends?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve paid attention to it like that. When I’m with my family, I’m usually trying to listen, or keep up with what the grandkids are saying—if they’re there, they talk so fast now. Half the time they’re showing me something on a phone and I can’t even tell where I’m supposed to look.\n\nWith friends, well… I don’t see as many people as I used to. Everybody gets older, people move, people get busy. We used to sit around after work sometimes, but that was years ago, and there was this diner that had pie in a glass case—lemon meringue, coconut cream, all that. You don’t see those places much anymore.\n\nSo, anxious with family or friends… I couldn’t really say “often.” I’m not sure I’d notice it unless someone pointed it out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you are not thinking about work or anything related to the warehouse?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. If I’m not thinking about work, I don’t know that I stop and check whether I’m anxious or not. Sometimes I’ll be thinking about something completely different, like whether the gutters need cleaning, or I’ll notice the neighbor’s dog barking and then I’m off remembering the dog we had when the kids were little.\n\nSo… when it’s not about the warehouse, I can’t really give you a clean yes or no. I haven’t noticed it clearly enough.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do for fun that you no longer want to do?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that. “Fun” changes a lot when you get older, doesn’t it? I used to do different things, sure, but some of that is just because life changes. You don’t run around the way you used to.\n\nI’ll start something sometimes and then not stick with it, but I can’t say if that’s because I don’t want to do it anymore or just because I get sidetracked. Like I’ll think about watching a game, and then I’m looking for the remote, and then I notice a stack of mail on the table. My wife says I leave things half done sometimes.\n\nSo… maybe there are things, but I haven’t really kept a list in my head of what I don’t want to do anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time, even when there's no apparent reason to be anxious?\n\nPatient: Most of the time? I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, sometimes I’ll feel… keyed up, I guess, and I couldn’t tell you exactly why. But then I’ll be sitting there and notice the clock is ticking loud, or I’ll remember I forgot to move the laundry, and then I’m thinking about that instead.\n\nIt’s hard to separate out what’s “no reason” and what’s just normal things piling up. My wife used to say I’d get wound up over little things, but then again she’d also say I couldn’t find the ketchup if it was right in front of me, so… I don’t know. I can’t give you a clean answer on “most of the time.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious for no specific reason when you are alone?\n\nPatient: When I’m alone… I don’t know. Sometimes the house feels quieter than I expect, and then I start noticing things, like the refrigerator humming or a car going by too slow, and I’ll wonder who that is, but then it’s probably just the neighbor’s son. He drives one of those loud little cars—my grandson was telling me they tune the exhaust on purpose now, which I don’t understand.\n\nBut anxious for no specific reason? I can’t really say. I don’t sit there and think, “I’m anxious now.” I just kind of move from one thing to the next, and by the time I’d figure it out, I’m thinking about something else. So… I’m not sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I came in because I’m not handling things right. They changed my hours at the warehouse, and then put in this new inventory system—computers and scanners and all that—and ever since, I feel like I’m just… coming apart.\n\nI used to be pretty dependable, you know. I’d show up, do my stocking, fill orders, keep my head down. I’ve always been that way—even back when I was younger, I took pride in being on time. But lately I’ve been missing shifts, and even missing things at church I said I’d help with. My wife says I’ve been snapping at everybody, and she’s not wrong.\n\nSome mornings I’ll get dressed, have my work boots on, and just sit there like I can’t make myself go in. It sounds foolish when I say it, but that place just feels like too much now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel nervous or on edge most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose I do, but it’s mostly tied to that warehouse situation. Ever since they changed my hours and put in that new inventory system with the scanners, I just feel like I’m falling apart.\n\nI mean, I’ll start thinking about going in and then my mind runs off—like whether I’ll mess up the orders, or if the supervisor’s watching. I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed it in a way I could point to. Maybe sometimes I feel uneasy, but I couldn’t tell you it’s happening often or what it’s tied to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're doing things that have nothing to do with the warehouse or your job?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m not really sure. If I’m doing something completely separate from work, like sitting at home or fiddling with something in the yard, I don’t always stop and think, “Am I anxious right now?” It’s more like I’m just doing the thing.\n\nSometimes my mind wanders off, though. I’ll be fixing a hinge or looking for a screwdriver, and then I remember my father had this old coffee can full of screws, all different sizes, and he somehow knew exactly which one he needed. I’ve got jars of them now, but half the time I can’t find the right one.\n\nSo… anxious during things that don’t have to do with the warehouse? I couldn’t say for sure. I haven’t really noticed it clearly enough to answer yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are relaxing or doing leisure activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Relaxing is a funny word, isn’t it? I sit in my chair sometimes, or I’ll put something on the television, but half the time I’m not paying much attention to it. My wife likes those cooking shows where they chop everything up ahead of time in little bowls. We never did that—my mother would just cut things right over the pot.\n\nBut anxious while I’m relaxing… I’m not sure I’d call it that. I don’t always notice what I’m feeling in the moment. Sometimes I’m just sitting there, and then I realize I’ve been staring at the same thing for a while. I couldn’t say for certain whether it’s anxiety or just me being older and distracted.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you are with your family or friends?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve paid attention to it like that. When I’m with my family, I’m usually trying to listen, or keep up with what the grandkids are saying—if they’re there, they talk so fast now. Half the time they’re showing me something on a phone and I can’t even tell where I’m supposed to look.\n\nWith friends, well… I don’t see as many people as I used to. Everybody gets older, people move, people get busy. We used to sit around after work sometimes, but that was years ago, and there was this diner that had pie in a glass case—lemon meringue, coconut cream, all that. You don’t see those places much anymore.\n\nSo, anxious with family or friends… I couldn’t really say “often.” I’m not sure I’d notice it unless someone pointed it out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you are not thinking about work or anything related to the warehouse?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. If I’m not thinking about work, I don’t know that I stop and check whether I’m anxious or not. Sometimes I’ll be thinking about something completely different, like whether the gutters need cleaning, or I’ll notice the neighbor’s dog barking and then I’m off remembering the dog we had when the kids were little.\n\nSo… when it’s not about the warehouse, I can’t really give you a clean yes or no. I haven’t noticed it clearly enough.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do for fun that you no longer want to do?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that. “Fun” changes a lot when you get older, doesn’t it? I used to do different things, sure, but some of that is just because life changes. You don’t run around the way you used to.\n\nI’ll start something sometimes and then not stick with it, but I can’t say if that’s because I don’t want to do it anymore or just because I get sidetracked. Like I’ll think about watching a game, and then I’m looking for the remote, and then I notice a stack of mail on the table. My wife says I leave things half done sometimes.\n\nSo… maybe there are things, but I haven’t really kept a list in my head of what I don’t want to do anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time, even when there's no apparent reason to be anxious?\n\nPatient: Most of the time? I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, sometimes I’ll feel… keyed up, I guess, and I couldn’t tell you exactly why. But then I’ll be sitting there and notice the clock is ticking loud, or I’ll remember I forgot to move the laundry, and then I’m thinking about that instead.\n\nIt’s hard to separate out what’s “no reason” and what’s just normal things piling up. My wife used to say I’d get wound up over little things, but then again she’d also say I couldn’t find the ketchup if it was right in front of me, so… I don’t know. I can’t give you a clean answer on “most of the time.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious for no specific reason when you are alone?\n\nPatient: When I’m alone… I don’t know. Sometimes the house feels quieter than I expect, and then I start noticing things, like the refrigerator humming or a car going by too slow, and I’ll wonder who that is, but then it’s probably just the neighbor’s son. He drives one of those loud little cars—my grandson was telling me they tune the exhaust on purpose now, which I don’t understand.\n\nBut anxious for no specific reason? I can’t really say. I don’t sit there and think, “I’m anxious now.” I just kind of move from one thing to the next, and by the time I’d figure it out, I’m thinking about something else. So… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed. I mean, my pants fit about the same, I think, though sometimes the belt feels different depending on which hole I use—funny how belts stretch out, or maybe that’s just old leather.\n\nI haven’t been weighing myself much. We have a scale somewhere, in the bathroom closet maybe, unless my wife moved it when she was cleaning out towels. So I can’t give you a number. But I wouldn’t say I’ve lost a significant amount, no.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. There was a box of old fishing gear out there the other day—my brother gave me some of that years back—and I started thinking about how we used to argue over who got the better rod. But I wouldn’t say I’ve lost a significant amount, no.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel anxious even when there are no problems or stressful situations present?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 74 / stocker_or_order_filler / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure. I mean, if there are no problems, then maybe I’m okay, but I don’t always know what counts as a “problem.” Like, I can be sitting there and then remember the mail didn’t come yet, or wonder if I left something in the wrong place at work—well, I’m not working like I used to, but I still think that way, you know, checking shelves, checking lists.

So… anxious with nothing going on? I can’t really tell. Sometimes I feel a little stirred up, but there’s usually something I can point to, even if it’s small. Or I go looking for something to explain it, I guess.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F41.0, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I came in because I’m not handling things right. They changed my hours at the warehouse, and then put in this new inventory system—computers and scanners and all that—and ever since, I feel like I’m just… coming apart.\n\nI used to be pretty dependable, you know. I’d show up, do my stocking, fill orders, keep my head down. I’ve always been that way—even back when I was younger, I took pride in being on time. But lately I’ve been missing shifts, and even missing things at church I said I’d help with. My wife says I’ve been snapping at everybody, and she’s not wrong.\n\nSome mornings I’ll get dressed, have my work boots on, and just sit there like I can’t make myself go in. It sounds foolish when I say it, but that place just feels like too much now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel nervous or on edge most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose I do, but it’s mostly tied to that warehouse situation. Ever since they changed my hours and put in that new inventory system with the scanners, I just feel like I’m falling apart.\n\nI mean, I’ll start thinking about going in and then my mind runs off—like whether I’ll mess up the orders, or if the supervisor’s watching. Then I think about how I used to never miss a shift, and now I’m missing work and even things at church I promised I’d help with. My wife says I’m short with everybody lately, and she’s right.\n\nSome mornings I’m sitting there in my work boots, just staring, wondering if I can even face the place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or hopeless when you're not thinking about work or the warehouse?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, when I’m away from work, sometimes I can sit with my wife or watch the game and be mostly alright… though my mind does wander back.\n\nHopeless, though… I don’t know. That’s a strong word. My father used to say people throw words around too easy—he worked hard right up until his knees gave out, and he never talked about feelings much. I guess I learned that from him.\n\nSo… I don’t really know if I feel sad when it’s not about the warehouse. I mostly just know that whole situation follows me around.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel nervous or on edge when you're not thinking about work or the warehouse?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed it separate from work, to be honest. It’s hard to pull the two apart, because once I’m home I’ll be sitting there and something small reminds me of the warehouse anyway.\n\nLike the other night my wife was asking where we keep the extra batteries, and I started thinking about those scanner batteries at work—how they never seem charged when you need them—and then I’m back there in my head. So… nervous when I’m not thinking about it? I’m not sure. I’d have to pay more attention, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day, even when you're not thinking about work or the warehouse?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say most of the day, no… or—I mean, I don’t really track it like that.\n\nSome days I feel worn down, but then I’ll get distracted by regular things, you know, fixing something around the house or talking with my wife, and it’s not like I’m sitting there crying all day. My mother used to keep a little notebook for everything—appointments, grocery prices, who called—and maybe if I did that I could tell you better. I just kind of go through the day.\n\nSo… sad most of the day when I’m not thinking about the warehouse? I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed it that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to enjoy doing that you don't find pleasurable anymore?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, I still do things, but whether I enjoy them the same way… I don’t know.\n\nI used to like tinkering around in the garage, but then again half the time I’d go out there just because something needed fixing. My neighbor had this old lawnmower once—wouldn’t start no matter what—and I spent an entire Saturday with him taking it apart. Funny thing is, it turned out to be something simple, like the fuel line, I think. He still brings that up sometimes.\n\nBut as far as not finding anything pleasurable anymore… I don’t know. I haven’t really sat down and measured it like that. Some things feel different, maybe, but I couldn’t tell you cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to look forward to that you no longer look forward to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I used to look forward to little things, I guess, but when you ask it that way my mind kind of goes blank.\n\nLike, there’s this diner my wife and I used to go to—well, we still go sometimes—out by the old hardware store. They changed owners a while back, and the coffee’s not the same. My wife says I’m too particular about coffee, and she’s probably right. My father was like that too, always complaining if it sat on the burner too long.\n\nBut whether I don’t look forward to things anymore… I don’t know. Some days I just don’t think that far ahead. I haven’t really paid attention to it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding places or situations outside of work because they remind you of the warehouse?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really thought of it like avoiding places.\n\nI mean, outside of work I still go where I need to go—store, gas station, church sometimes if my wife’s going. Though there are places with those big loading bays, you know, the kind behind grocery stores, and they all sort of look the same after a while. Funny thing, when I was younger I worked a summer at a place that smelled like cardboard and floor cleaner, and every time I smell that same kind of cleaner I think of it.\n\nBut avoiding them because of the warehouse… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve been keeping track of that. I might go the long way sometimes just out of habit, but I couldn’t say it’s because of that exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you no longer enjoy activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to say it. I mean, I still do things I used to do, but whether I enjoy them like before… I haven’t kept track of it that way.\n\nSometimes I’ll start something, like sorting through stuff in the garage, and then I end up just moving the same box from one side to the other. There was a box of old fishing gear out there the other day—my brother gave me some of that years back—and I started thinking about how we used to argue over who got the better rod. He always said I tangled the line on purpose, which wasn’t true.\n\nSo, do I no longer enjoy things? I’m not sure. Some things don’t feel the same maybe, but I couldn’t give you a straight yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed anything significant. My pants fit about the same, I think, though some of them are old work pants and they’ve been stretched out for years.\n\nMy wife bought a scale a while back, one of those digital ones that flashes numbers at you, but I don’t get on it much. I remember my mother had one with the little needle, and you had to tap it with your foot to make it go back to zero. Those were simpler, I guess.\n\nBut, no… I can’t say I’ve noticed a big change in my weight recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you are not thinking about work or the warehouse?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out that cleanly. I mean, if I’m not thinking about work, I’m usually thinking about something else—like bills, or whether I locked the back door, or some little thing my wife asked me to pick up and I forgot until I’m already sitting down.\n\nAnxious… that’s a word people use more now than they used to, I think. When I was younger we just said we were “wound up” or “had nerves,” like my aunt used to say. She’d keep peppermint candies in her purse and offer you one if you looked upset, as if that fixed everything.\n\nSo, do I often feel anxious when I’m not thinking about the warehouse? I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed it in a way I could point to. Maybe sometimes I feel uneasy, but I couldn’t tell you it’s happening often or what it’s tied to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're doing things that have nothing to do with the warehouse or your job?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m not really sure. If I’m doing something completely separate from work, like sitting at home or fiddling with something in the yard, I don’t always stop and think, “Am I anxious right now?” It’s more like I’m just doing the thing.\n\nSometimes my mind wanders off, though. I’ll be fixing a hinge or looking for a screwdriver, and then I remember my father had this old coffee can full of screws, all different sizes, and he somehow knew exactly which one he needed. I’ve got jars of them now, but half the time I can’t find the right one.\n\nSo… anxious during things that don’t have to do with the warehouse? I couldn’t say for sure. I haven’t really noticed it clearly enough to answer yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are relaxing or doing leisure activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Relaxing is a funny word, isn’t it? I sit in my chair sometimes, or I’ll put something on the television, but half the time I’m not paying much attention to it. My wife likes those cooking shows where they chop everything up ahead of time in little bowls. We never did that—my mother would just cut things right over the pot.\n\nBut anxious while I’m relaxing… I’m not sure I’d call it that. I don’t always notice what I’m feeling in the moment. Sometimes I’m just sitting there, and then I realize I’ve been staring at the same thing for a while. I couldn’t say for certain whether it’s anxiety or just me being older and distracted.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you are with your family or friends?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve paid attention to it like that. When I’m with my family, I’m usually trying to listen, or keep up with what the grandkids are saying—if they’re there, they talk so fast now. Half the time they’re showing me something on a phone and I can’t even tell where I’m supposed to look.\n\nWith friends, well… I don’t see as many people as I used to. Everybody gets older, people move, people get busy. We used to sit around after work sometimes, but that was years ago, and there was this diner that had pie in a glass case—lemon meringue, coconut cream, all that. You don’t see those places much anymore.\n\nSo, anxious with family or friends… I couldn’t really say “often.” I’m not sure I’d notice it unless someone pointed it out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you are not thinking about work or anything related to the warehouse?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. If I’m not thinking about work, I don’t know that I stop and check whether I’m anxious or not. Sometimes I’ll be thinking about something completely different, like whether the gutters need cleaning, or I’ll notice the neighbor’s dog barking and then I’m off remembering the dog we had when the kids were little.\n\nSo… when it’s not about the warehouse, I can’t really give you a clean yes or no. I haven’t noticed it clearly enough.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do for fun that you no longer want to do?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that. “Fun” changes a lot when you get older, doesn’t it? I used to do different things, sure, but some of that is just because life changes. You don’t run around the way you used to.\n\nI’ll start something sometimes and then not stick with it, but I can’t say if that’s because I don’t want to do it anymore or just because I get sidetracked. Like I’ll think about watching a game, and then I’m looking for the remote, and then I notice a stack of mail on the table. My wife says I leave things half done sometimes.\n\nSo… maybe there are things, but I haven’t really kept a list in my head of what I don’t want to do anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time, even when there's no apparent reason to be anxious?\n\nPatient: Most of the time? I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, sometimes I’ll feel… keyed up, I guess, and I couldn’t tell you exactly why. But then I’ll be sitting there and notice the clock is ticking loud, or I’ll remember I forgot to move the laundry, and then I’m thinking about that instead.\n\nIt’s hard to separate out what’s “no reason” and what’s just normal things piling up. My wife used to say I’d get wound up over little things, but then again she’d also say I couldn’t find the ketchup if it was right in front of me, so… I don’t know. I can’t give you a clean answer on “most of the time.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious for no specific reason when you are alone?\n\nPatient: When I’m alone… I don’t know. Sometimes the house feels quieter than I expect, and then I start noticing things, like the refrigerator humming or a car going by too slow, and I’ll wonder who that is, but then it’s probably just the neighbor’s son. He drives one of those loud little cars—my grandson was telling me they tune the exhaust on purpose now, which I don’t understand.\n\nBut anxious for no specific reason? I can’t really say. I don’t sit there and think, “I’m anxious now.” I just kind of move from one thing to the next, and by the time I’d figure it out, I’m thinking about something else. So… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed. I mean, my pants fit about the same, I think, though sometimes the belt feels different depending on which hole I use—funny how belts stretch out, or maybe that’s just old leather.\n\nI haven’t been weighing myself much. We have a scale somewhere, in the bathroom closet maybe, unless my wife moved it when she was cleaning out towels. So I can’t give you a number. But I wouldn’t say I’ve lost a significant amount, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious even when there are no problems or stressful situations present?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, if there are no problems, then maybe I’m okay, but I don’t always know what counts as a “problem.” Like, I can be sitting there and then remember the mail didn’t come yet, or wonder if I left something in the wrong place at work—well, I’m not working like I used to, but I still think that way, you know, checking shelves, checking lists.\n\nSo… anxious with nothing going on? I can’t really tell. Sometimes I feel a little stirred up, but there’s usually something I can point to, even if it’s small. Or I go looking for something to explain it, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Maybe sometimes I feel uneasy, but I couldn’t tell you it’s happening often or what it’s tied to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're doing things that have nothing to do with the warehouse or your job?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m not really sure. If I’m doing something completely separate from work, like sitting at home or fiddling with something in the yard, I don’t always stop and think, “Am I anxious right now?” It’s more like I’m just doing the thing.\n\nSometimes my mind wanders off, though. I’ll be fixing a hinge or looking for a screwdriver, and then I remember my father had this old coffee can full of screws, all different sizes, and he somehow knew exactly which one he needed. I’ve got jars of them now, but half the time I can’t find the right one.\n\nSo… anxious during things that don’t have to do with the warehouse? I couldn’t say for sure. I haven’t really noticed it clearly enough to answer yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are relaxing or doing leisure activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Relaxing is a funny word, isn’t it? I sit in my chair sometimes, or I’ll put something on the television, but half the time I’m not paying much attention to it. My wife likes those cooking shows where they chop everything up ahead of time in little bowls. We never did that—my mother would just cut things right over the pot.\n\nBut anxious while I’m relaxing… I’m not sure I’d call it that. I don’t always notice what I’m feeling in the moment. Sometimes I’m just sitting there, and then I realize I’ve been staring at the same thing for a while. I couldn’t say for certain whether it’s anxiety or just me being older and distracted.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you are with your family or friends?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve paid attention to it like that. When I’m with my family, I’m usually trying to listen, or keep up with what the grandkids are saying—if they’re there, they talk so fast now. Half the time they’re showing me something on a phone and I can’t even tell where I’m supposed to look.\n\nWith friends, well… I don’t see as many people as I used to. Everybody gets older, people move, people get busy. We used to sit around after work sometimes, but that was years ago, and there was this diner that had pie in a glass case—lemon meringue, coconut cream, all that. You don’t see those places much anymore.\n\nSo, anxious with family or friends… I couldn’t really say “often.” I’m not sure I’d notice it unless someone pointed it out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you are not thinking about work or anything related to the warehouse?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. If I’m not thinking about work, I don’t know that I stop and check whether I’m anxious or not. Sometimes I’ll be thinking about something completely different, like whether the gutters need cleaning, or I’ll notice the neighbor’s dog barking and then I’m off remembering the dog we had when the kids were little.\n\nSo… when it’s not about the warehouse, I can’t really give you a clean yes or no. I haven’t noticed it clearly enough.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do for fun that you no longer want to do?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that. “Fun” changes a lot when you get older, doesn’t it? I used to do different things, sure, but some of that is just because life changes. You don’t run around the way you used to.\n\nI’ll start something sometimes and then not stick with it, but I can’t say if that’s because I don’t want to do it anymore or just because I get sidetracked. Like I’ll think about watching a game, and then I’m looking for the remote, and then I notice a stack of mail on the table. My wife says I leave things half done sometimes.\n\nSo… maybe there are things, but I haven’t really kept a list in my head of what I don’t want to do anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time, even when there's no apparent reason to be anxious?\n\nPatient: Most of the time? I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, sometimes I’ll feel… keyed up, I guess, and I couldn’t tell you exactly why. But then I’ll be sitting there and notice the clock is ticking loud, or I’ll remember I forgot to move the laundry, and then I’m thinking about that instead.\n\nIt’s hard to separate out what’s “no reason” and what’s just normal things piling up. My wife used to say I’d get wound up over little things, but then again she’d also say I couldn’t find the ketchup if it was right in front of me, so… I don’t know. I can’t give you a clean answer on “most of the time.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious for no specific reason when you are alone?\n\nPatient: When I’m alone… I don’t know. Sometimes the house feels quieter than I expect, and then I start noticing things, like the refrigerator humming or a car going by too slow, and I’ll wonder who that is, but then it’s probably just the neighbor’s son. He drives one of those loud little cars—my grandson was telling me they tune the exhaust on purpose now, which I don’t understand.\n\nBut anxious for no specific reason? I can’t really say. I don’t sit there and think, “I’m anxious now.” I just kind of move from one thing to the next, and by the time I’d figure it out, I’m thinking about something else. So… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed. I mean, my pants fit about the same, I think, though sometimes the belt feels different depending on which hole I use—funny how belts stretch out, or maybe that’s just old leather.\n\nI haven’t been weighing myself much. We have a scale somewhere, in the bathroom closet maybe, unless my wife moved it when she was cleaning out towels. So I can’t give you a number. But I wouldn’t say I’ve lost a significant amount, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious even when there are no problems or stressful situations present?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, if there are no problems, then maybe I’m okay, but I don’t always know what counts as a “problem.” Like, I can be sitting there and then remember the mail didn’t come yet, or wonder if I left something in the wrong place at work—well, I’m not working like I used to, but I still think that way, you know, checking shelves, checking lists.\n\nSo… anxious with nothing going on? I can’t really tell. Sometimes I feel a little stirred up, but there’s usually something I can point to, even if it’s small. Or I go looking for something to explain it, I guess."
